**Assistant — Turn 32**

This final Haruko batch is less about new “major plot events” and more about **making Haruko’s personality mechanically continuous**. The card stories gave us Haruko’s big wounds: Mana, obscurity, delayed success, romance deferred, age anxiety, Makino as long-term witness. These message/bond transcripts show how all of that lives in her everyday speech.

The cleanest capstone formulation is:

> **Haruko is a delayed-blooming idol whose ordinary life never stops pressing against her idol life. She wants to be sparkling, youthful, desirable, competent, funny, useful, and emotionally necessary — but because she spent so long as the “plain,” struggling, almost-forgotten idol, she needs constant reassurance that her warmth, hobbies, domesticity, age, body, and accumulated experience are not disqualifications from idolhood, but the exact materials from which her idolhood is made.**

## 1\. Messages: Haruko Part 1 — card aftercare, recurring insecurities, and Makino as the person who validates “Haruko as Haruko”

Part 1 mostly functions as post-card aftercare. These are not usually new arcs, but they are valuable because they show which feelings linger after the card events end. The strongest recurring point is that Haruko keeps returning to the question: **“Is the ordinary version of me enough?”** message\_message\_group\_hrk\_001\_m…

The 「beyond the goal」 message is very useful. Haruko worries that being called “気さくで親しみやすい” — approachable and friendly — means she lacks true top-idol aura. Staff see her as a “近所の素敵なお姉さん” — a lovely older sister from the neighborhood — and she worries this means she lacks BIG4 force. But Makino reframes this as her strength. This is a micro-version of her whole arc: she sees warmth as insufficient stardom; Makino tells her warmth is her stardom.

The 「解き放たれる十七歳」 follow-up is also revealing because she tries to manufacture the blush that fans liked. That failure matters. Haruko’s charm cannot be faked as a tactic. Her best idol appeal comes when her real embarrassment, warmth, and overthinking leak through naturally. It reinforces a key point: Haruko is not a persona genius like kana. Her “weapon” is sincerity that becomes funny despite itself.

The 「共に繋いだ約束の音」 message shows Haruko as an institutional connector. After the special Hoshimi live, she proposes more cross-group training because different groups can teach each other. This fits her “social glue” role: Haruko does not only care inside Sunny Peace; she notices how the agency as a whole can become more mutually nourishing.

The 「私、決めたよ」 message is almost painfully Haruko: she drafts praise lines for Makino to use on her — “17歳より17歳に見えるアイドル,” “多趣味,” “家庭的で嫁にもらうにはもってこい.” She is joking, but the joke reveals her hunger. She wants to be told that her age, hobbies, and domesticity are not “plainness” but charm. The phrase 家庭的 (domestic / good at household care) especially matters because Haruko fears it makes her too motherly, but also knows it is one of her real powers.

The Mana birthday message further reinforces that Haruko is one of the memory-keepers. She wants to continue celebrating Mana’s birthday, and she says Makino being absent from Mana’s birthday would be strange. Haruko understands that Mana’s memory is communal, and that Makino is part of that ritual world.

The 温泉 follow-up about さん付け (the “-san” honorific) is minor but excellent. Haruko notices that Makino calls many others by name but still uses “Haruko-san” for her. She first questions the distance, then decides it gives her specialness: “Manager君のさん付けは私のもの.” This is very Haruko — she turns status anxiety into a private intimacy marker.

The birthday messages matter too. Across them, she gets overwhelmed by pure praise, jokes about being a タヌキ (a sly fox/raccoon-dog type), removes candles from her cake, worries Makino is too busy or “popular” with the other girls to watch her, then feels relieved when he gives her something perfectly chosen. The recurring birthday motif is: **Haruko wants to be celebrated, but gets embarrassed when celebration proves someone has truly seen her.**

**Contribution:** Part 1 confirms that Haruko’s card-event issues are not temporary. Age, aura, domesticity, specialness with Makino, Mana’s memory, and being “seen” remain active in her everyday thought-patterns.

## 2\. Messages: Haruko Part 2 — Haruko’s daily operating system: praise, food, rivals, plainness, and grassroots idolhood

Part 2 is probably the best “baseline Haruko voice” file. It is full of small, low-stakes messages, but together they show how Haruko’s mind moves when she is not inside a major event story. message\_message\_group\_hrk\_001\_m…

The first pattern is **performance reassurance**. She asks how yesterday’s live looked, says her body felt light, and is delighted that Makino noticed. She does not just want generic praise; she wants evidence that what she felt internally reached someone externally. “伝わってるー！感激！” — “It got through! I’m moved!” — is a tiny but perfect Haruko line. She wants the bridge between inner effort and outer reception to be confirmed.

Second, Haruko constantly reverses care onto Makino. She asks what he wants because she wants to reward him; she catches him napping and secretly covers him with a blanket; she buys sweets for him; she wants to invite him to taste pickles or eat rice; she scolds him to show up at jobs because the girls lose energy when he is absent. This is not just flirtation. It is her care-language. But because it is Makino, it often acquires romantic warmth.

Third, she articulates her group-idol thesis very clearly. In “アイドルって面白いわね,” she says she was probably not suited for solo work, that it was lonely, and that the pre-live huddle suits her. The key line is that some idols shine alone, while others shine by gathering with others. That is essentially Sunny Peace’s philosophy in miniature. Haruko’s delayed success is not just “she became better”; it is that she found the right ecology.

Fourth, the messages keep her competitive anxiety alive. LizNoir and TRINITYAiLE still scare her because their level makes her feel Sunny Peace may be left behind. But she also converts that fear into fuel. This is a mature Haruko trait: she may spiral briefly, but she usually reconstitutes anxiety into effort rather than resentment.

Fifth, her self-image wound remains comic but serious. She calls herself 地味 (plain), worries even her conversation topics are plain, jokes that “業と書いてカルマ” — “write it as karma” — defines her plainness. Yet the messages around pickles, grassroots events, signatures, and local fan interaction reveal that her “plain” interests are not empty; they are exactly why she understands direct, small-scale, person-to-person idol work.

The “草の根的な活動” phrase is important: 草の根的 (grassroots) means ground-level, local, direct activity. Haruko likes signings and handshake-style events because fans give direct words. That is not secondary to idolhood for her. She values the small circuit of encouragement: fan speaks, idol receives, idol continues, idol gives back.

**Contribution:** Part 2 makes Haruko’s daily personality coherent. She is praise-hungry but generous, insecure but resilient, domestic but professional, competitive but non-bitter, and deeply suited to group idolhood because she naturally thinks in terms of reciprocal support.

## 3\. Messages: Haruko Part 3 — university life, job hunting anxiety, “all average,” and the desire to be allowed to lean

Part 3 is one of the most important message files because it brings Haruko’s university/adult-life pressure into full view. message\_message\_group\_hrk\_001\_m…

The instant messages at the start are some of the clearest Makino-romantic-comedy material. A male classmate at university says he is her fan, and Haruko complains that Makino does not react with jealousy: she wanted something like “悪い虫” — a bad bug, meaning an unwanted suitor — or “俺の遙子が遠くに行ってしまう” — “my Haruko is going away.” Makino instead says he trusts her, which she calls a 殺し文句 (killer line / heart-stopping phrase). This is textbook Haruko: she tries to provoke romantic possessiveness, then gets hit harder by sincere trust.

Her “甘えたい” message is perhaps the most direct non-card statement of her caretaker fatigue. 甘える (to lean on someone / be indulged / allow oneself to be dependent) is central to Haruko. She likes being Sunny Peace’s お姉さん (older-sister figure), but says that being everyone’s older sister is tiring and that sometimes she wants someone to let her lean. Makino gives exactly the response she wanted, and she calls that ツーカー — a tacit, easy mutual understanding.

The 就活戦線 (job-hunting front) mini-arc is major. 就活 (job hunting) is a normal university-life threshold, and Haruko’s friends entering that world forces the question she usually defers: should she be thinking about ordinary employment, marriage, and conventional adulthood? A friend asks whether she will “普通に就職して、結婚” — get a normal job and think about marriage — and Haruko wonders whether she left something important behind. Makino answers that he likes “idol Haruko” and “stage Haruko,” but also says he would support her if she considered job hunting. That matters because he does not trap her in idolhood; he supports her agency. Haruko then decides to declare at university that she is continuing as an idol full-force. The key is not that she rejects ordinary life because it is worthless. It is that she chooses idolhood consciously, after feeling the pressure of ordinary adulthood.

The オール平均点 (all-around average score) arc is also major. Haruko sees Mana and Sakura as ナチュラルボーンキラキラ — natural-born sparkle — and names her feelings as 羨望 and 嫉妬 (envy and jealousy). She then asks what she has that they do not. Experience? Technique? Talk ability? But she panics that this only amounts to being “オール平均点” — all average, no standout weapon. The resolution, implied through later messages, is that Haruko’s value is not one dazzling specialty but situational competence: when there is a staff mistake or unstable situation, she can step in calmly. Her “average” is actually broad survivability.

There is also a strong adult/domestic thread: she invites Makino to drink wine because he will care for her if she gets drunk, makes lunches, worries about her grandmother’s perception of her idol career, and gets excited about male fans at university while still needing Makino to remind her not to lose 初心 (beginner’s heart / original humility).

**Contribution:** Part 3 deepens Haruko’s adult crossroads. She is not merely “older than the others”; she is actively negotiating university life, ordinary adulthood, marriage expectations, male attention, Makino attachment, and whether being an idol is still a legitimate life path.

## 4\. Messages: Haruko Part 4 — technical correction, hobby-to-work conversion, “ruins idol,” and the wound of lost seventeen

Part 4 turns several of Haruko’s comic motifs into structured mini-arcs. message\_message\_group\_hrk\_001\_m…

The ダンスのキレ (dance sharpness / crispness) arc is important because it refuses to let Haruko be only “the warm one.” She trains more, stays late, and tries to sharpen her dancing, but Rei bluntly says her キレ is down and asks whether she gained weight. Haruko panics, but the actual issue is overtraining and inefficient self-directed practice. A dance coach explains that repeated load on the same areas has thrown off her balance. This is a useful contrast with Rei: Haruko’s answer is not “more effort” but “better structured effort.” She learns that mature professionalism includes coaching, recovery, nutrition, and efficiency.

The camp-work arc shows her hobby becoming professional speech. Haruko worries she has too much to say, fears being a 面倒なアイドル (troublesome / high-maintenance idol), asks for advance alignment, and then realizes the interview succeeds precisely because of preparation. This is one of the clearest “ordinary hobby becomes idol weapon” arcs. She is not becoming less weird; she is learning how to package weirdness responsibly.

The 小悪魔マネージャー arc around 廃墟巡り (ruin exploration / visiting abandoned places) is excellent Haruko comedy. She wants more work that uses her niche hobbies, pushes Makino about ruins, tries to invite him on her day off, gets disappointed when he is busy, then later complains he treats her casually — only to learn he had been arranging a ruins-related job for her. She calls this ツンデレ (prickly outside, affectionate inside) and even “小悪魔” (little devil / teasing charmer). The joke lands because Makino’s practical support is genuinely romantic-coded in Haruko’s emotional grammar: he seems absent, but he is actually listening and acting.

The キラキラの17歳 (sparkling seventeen) arc returns to the deepest age wound. Haruko envies Chisa’s real seventeen-year-old status, wants to wear a school uniform, and wishes to return to “キラキラの17歳” — a glittering, sparkling seventeen. The job Makino finds appears to be a student/uniform role, but she learns she is actually cast as a working adult eldest sister, even with older actresses playing her younger sisters. That is cruelly funny, but thematically precise: the industry keeps reading Haruko as mature, not fresh. She first mourns lost freshness, then tries to pivot into “sexy adult charm.” The arc does not erase her wound; it shows her trying to metabolize it into another performance angle.

**Contribution:** Part 4 shows Haruko learning to professionalize herself without losing herself. Her body, hobbies, age, and niche interests are not embarrassments to hide; they are materials that require framing, support, and craft.

## 5\. Bond stories: Haruko — social texture outside Makino, and Haruko as “ordinary absurdity”

The bond file is mostly comic, but it is extremely useful because it shows Haruko outside the Makino axis. It places her with fran, Kokoro, and Aoi, and it makes her feel less like “Makino’s warm quasi-romantic older-sister idol” and more like a socially chaotic woman with her own strange ecology. bond\_hrk\_001\_hrk

The first bond story is Haruko fantasizing about university circles. She imagines camp-circle青春 (youthful college life), magic-circle glamor, sports-manager romance, being confessed to, and then saying she has someone already in her heart. It is funny, but it directly supports the larger thesis: Haruko missed many ordinary youth scripts and now lives them through imagination, roleplay, or performance.

The fran ruin story is excellent because Haruko’s 廃墟 (ruins / abandoned buildings) interest clashes with fran’s capitalist aesthetic sense. Haruko sees romance, history, decay, and atmosphere; fran sees dirt, danger, ghosts, and possible monetization. Haruko then proposes “廃ドル” — a portmanteau of 廃墟 and idol, roughly “ruins idol.” It is silly, but it captures Haruko’s evolving professional logic: even the most un-idol-like hobby might become a new idol genre if handled with enough enthusiasm.

The drunken fran stories are useful because they expose Haruko’s weak point: when lonely or excited, she becomes clingy, careless, and dependent. She invites fran to dinner after a university friend cancels, gets drunk, forgets her wallet, calls miho, falls asleep, and later has to be delivered to the dorm. This is Haruko with the responsible older-sister mask dissolved. She is not only caretaker; she is also the one who begs “置いてかないでぇ～！” — “Don’t leave me behind!” That line is comic, but emotionally consistent with her fear of being left behind by Mana, by youth, by success, by peers, by ordinary life.

The Kokoro uniform story is another age/body comedy piece. Haruko tries to fit into a school uniform skirt, insists it is not a question of whether it fits but that she will make it fit, and finally succeeds through heroic physical compression. The joke is broad, but it reinforces how intensely she wants to prove that age has not excluded her from “schoolgirl sparkle.”

The finger-dance story is pure absurdity, but useful. Haruko has a bizarre private hobby, practices dance choreography with her fingers, and can perform it with shocking technical precision. That says something real: even her strange hobbies contain discipline. The line between “Haruko being weird” and “Haruko turning weirdness into craft” is very thin.

The ぬか床 (rice-bran pickling bed) and ぬか漬け (rice-bran pickles) story may be the funniest encapsulation of Haruko’s domestic chaos. She lovingly tends her pickling bed like a pet, sings to it, then discovers random things she drunkenly pickled: apple, cream cheese, chicken tenderloin. It ties together alcohol, food, domesticity, body anxiety, and her relationships with fran, Rio, and miho.

The Aoi story, where Haruko worries whether she has gotten fat after cooking/eating fried chicken, smoothies, late-night ice cream, and Aoi’s odd observations, extends the “body as idol instrument” theme. Haruko’s body anxiety is not simply vanity; it sits at the intersection of appetite, cooking, youth, sensuality, and idol presentation.

**Contribution:** The bond file is lower in major plot weight but very high in “personhood” value. It makes Haruko messier, weirder, lonelier, funnier, and more embodied.

## What this final batch changes

The biggest refinement is that Haruko’s core conflict is not only “I was late to bloom.” It is:

> **I bloomed late, so how do I keep living as an idol while the ordinary milestones I missed keep returning as ghosts?**

Those ghosts are everywhere: 就活 (job hunting), 結婚 (marriage), 制服 (school uniform), 17歳 (seventeen), デート (dates), サークル (college circles), 初々しさ (freshness), ordinary male attention, birthdays, body changes, and being treated as an adult instead of a girl. Haruko does not simply reject these things. She converts them into comedy, work, roleplay, cooking, wine, performance, or private conversation with Makino.

The second refinement is that Haruko’s “plainness” is now clearly a false self-diagnosis. She calls herself 地味 (plain), オール平均点 (all-around average), lacking aura, lacking freshness, and too domestic. But the messages show the opposite: she has a dense, specific identity. Camping, ruins, pickles, cooking, wine, puzzles, grassroots fan interaction, caretaker competence, emotional literacy, and group support are not random quirks. They are her idol vocabulary.

The third refinement is Makino’s role. In the card stories, Makino was already her witness, manager, and romantic-coded partner. In the messages, he becomes her everyday emotional regulator. She uses him for praise, teasing, consultation, date hypotheticals, job anxiety, age anxiety, body anxiety, ordinary adulthood anxiety, and soft dependency. The phrase 甘える (to lean on / be indulged) is the key: Haruko is the person everyone leans on, but Makino is the person she asks to let her lean.

## Priority ranking

**Core / essential for Haruko:**
Messages Part 3, especially 就活戦線 and オール平均点. These directly define Haruko’s adult crossroads and her “ordinary vs idol life” anxiety. message\_message\_group\_hrk\_001\_m…

**Very important:**
Messages Part 4, especially ダンスのキレ, キャンプのお仕事, 小悪魔マネージャー, and キラキラの17歳. This file shows her converting body anxiety, hobbies, niche identity, and age wounds into professional form. message\_message\_group\_hrk\_001\_m…

**Very important for baseline voice:**
Messages Part 2. This is the best file for Haruko’s day-to-day rhythm: praise hunger, care, rivalry, grassroots idolhood, food, plainness jokes, and Makino trust. message\_message\_group\_hrk\_001\_m…

**Important aftercare:**
Messages Part 1. It reinforces earlier card-event conclusions, especially BIG4 aura anxiety, specialness with Makino, Mana birthday rituals, romance follow-ups, and birthday vulnerability. message\_message\_group\_hrk\_001\_m…

**High-value texture, lower plot weight:**
Bond stories. These are indispensable for Haruko’s weirdness, social comedy, non-Makino relationships, body/food/alcohol texture, and the proof that her domestic persona is lively rather than flat. bond\_hrk\_001\_hrk

## Final updated Haruko thesis

> **Saeki Haruko is Sunny Peace’s delayed-blooming ordinary miracle: the idol who was not chosen early, who watched brighter girls from below, who lacked the obvious sparkle of Mana or Sakura, and who spent years fearing that she was merely plain, average, domestic, older, and useful. Her mature arc is the discovery that these supposed disqualifications are her actual idol materials. She shines through care, persistence, grassroots contact, food, hobbies, social warmth, professional experience, and the ability to keep going without becoming bitter.**
>
> **The final transcript layer makes her more adult and more vulnerable. Haruko is not only the group’s お姉さん (older-sister figure); she is also a woman who missed ordinary romance, envies lost seventeen-year-old sparkle, worries about job hunting and marriage, wants to be seen as desirable, wants someone safe to lean on, and still chooses idolhood because it is where she feels most alive. Makino matters because he is the person who has watched all versions of her: the failed audition girl, the lonely solo idol, the caretaker, the drunk mess, the domestic cook, the date-roleplay partner, the BIG4 idol, and the woman who still wants to be someone’s first.**
>
> **Haruko’s idolhood is not brilliance descending from heaven. It is warmth accumulated through years of not giving up.**

Across all these transcripts, how would you assess Haruko's level of romantic interest in the manager (Makino Kouhei)? If present, how much does his past with Mana Nagase complicate those feelings?

**Assistant — Turn 34**

I would assess Haruko’s romantic interest in Makino as **highly present, textually deliberate, and more developed than Rei’s**, but still kept in the soft “idol-game affection route” zone rather than sealed as a formal confession.

The cleanest formulation:

> **Haruko is not merely fond of Makino. She has a long-running, romantically charged attachment to him that began as adolescent憧れ — admiration/crush-like awareness — and matured into adult trust, domestic intimacy, flirtation, and a desire to be personally chosen.**

## Level of romantic interest: high, but carefully deniable

I would put it roughly like this:

**Explicit confirmed confession:** moderate-low.
**Romantic-coded interest:** very high.
**Long-term emotional dependence / trust:** extremely high.
**Haruko herself consciously understands the romantic shape of her feelings:** higher than Rei, but still partially evasive.
**Likelihood that she would pursue him in a world without idol/manager constraints and Mana’s shadow:** high.

Haruko’s evidence is much stronger than “she blushes sometimes.” It has several layers.

First, the Valentine card outright says she once “ちょっと意識していた人” — someone she was a little conscious of — and that person was Makino. The flashback has all the structure of an early crush: she notices that he knows her schedule, wonders if he is paying attention to her despite being Mana’s manager, gets flustered by his sincere encouragement, makes Valentine chocolate for a man other than her father for the first time, cannot bring herself to give it, and finally passes it off as 義理 (obligation/friendship chocolate) despite the scene clearly undercutting that excuse. card\_hrk\_016\_st-card-hrk-05-vln…

Second, her later romantic behavior toward Makino is not accidental. She repeatedly asks him for 恋人ごっこ (pretend-lover roleplay), including the hot-spring boyfriend scenario, the at-home date, the cafe date, shopping date, and wedding/drama practice. These are framed as work practice, but Haruko clearly enjoys the romantic pretext, gets flustered when Makino plays along too well, and repeatedly wants the experience to continue beyond its practical purpose. card\_hrk\_012\_st-card-hrk-05-one… card\_hrk\_014\_st-card-hrk-05-paj… card\_hrk\_017\_st-card-hrk-05-wed…

Third, her ideal恋愛 (romance/love) profile maps heavily onto Makino. In the home-date magazine card, she says her ideal partner is someone she can relax with, lean on, and feel safely cared for. That is almost exactly how the surrounding story frames Makino: the person she cooks with, falls asleep near, confides in, and trusts to support her without making her feel weak. card\_hrk\_014\_st-card-hrk-05-paj…

Fourth, the wedding card goes beyond playful teasing. The drama role is about 片思い (one-sided love), and Haruko ends the card wanting not merely to be ranked as “the idol people want as a bride,” but someday to become 好きな人の一番 — the number one person of someone she loves. That line comes after Makino has stood in as a groom-like figure and after she privately wants to monopolize his back a little longer. It is not a formal confession, but the staging is doing everything short of underlining Makino’s name. card\_hrk\_017\_st-card-hrk-05-wed…

Fifth, the messages show her actively seeking signs of possessiveness, exclusivity, and special status from him. She jokes that when a male college fan approaches her, Makino should react with “俺の遙子が遠くに行ってしまう” — “my Haruko is going away” — then gets hit hard when he says he trusts her. She also drunkenly tells her college friends that Makino is “遙子のもの” — Haruko’s — and that she will not hand him over. message\_message\_group\_hrk\_001\_m… message\_message\_group\_hrk\_001\_m…

So I would not call this merely “manager affection.” It is romantic-coded attraction plus long-term companionship plus domestic trust.

## Why Haruko is more conscious than Rei

Rei’s Makino interest often appears as failed composure: she gets seen, panics, denies, scolds. Haruko is different. Haruko knows what “date,” “boyfriend,” “lover practice,” “jealousy,” “bride,” “number one,” and “romance” mean. She is not naïve about the frame.

Her evasions are therefore not “I have no idea what this feeling is.” They are more like:

> **“I know this has a romantic shape, but I can keep it safe by calling it practice, work, teasing, drinking, or nostalgia.”**

That is why Haruko feels more adult. Rei’s affection is like emotional exposure by accident. Haruko’s is like emotional exposure disguised as a bit.

## How Mana complicates it

Mana complicates Haruko’s feelings enormously, but not in a simple “love triangle” way.

The complication is that Haruko’s feelings for Makino formed inside a world where Makino was already narratively and emotionally attached to Mana. Haruko noticed him when he was “Mana’s manager.” She admired him, relied on him, and received encouragement from him, but she also understood that Mana occupied the central place in his professional and emotional life. In the early flashbacks, Makino’s identity is inseparable from managing Mana, while Haruko is the struggling idol watching Mana’s back from below. card\_hrk\_016\_st-card-hrk-05-vln…

That creates at least four complications.

### 1\. Haruko’s feelings begin under Mana’s shadow

Haruko is not just “a girl who likes Makino.” She is a girl who liked or became conscious of Makino while she was also watching Mana become the star she could not become. Makino’s attention mattered partly because attention from “Mana’s manager” felt like proof that Haruko was visible at all.

That makes his praise unusually powerful. When he tells her after an audition failure that she is already a wonderful idol and that her charm simply has not reached people yet, it is not only romantic encouragement. It is existential validation from the person closest to the miracle.

### 2\. Haruko knows Makino’s grief and does not want to overwrite it

Haruko is one of the characters who most understands Mana as a living person, not only a myth. She helped prepare Mana’s birthday, knows Makino belongs at Mana’s birthday commemorations, and later sings 「voyage」 as a song that links Mana’s encouragement to Haruko’s future. Haruko does not treat Mana as a rival to be beaten romantically. She treats Mana as a beloved, impossible absence. card\_hrk\_011\_st-card-hrk-05-mna… message\_message\_group\_hrk\_001\_m…

That means Haruko’s affection for Makino is constrained by reverence. To pursue him too directly could feel, emotionally, like stepping into a space still occupied by Mana’s memory.

### 3\. The “I want to be someone’s first” line carries Mana’s ghost

When Haruko says she wants to be 好きな人の一番 — the number one of someone she loves — it lands harder because Makino has a past where Mana was arguably his central girl, central idol, central wound, and central unfinished relationship. Even if one does not read Makino/Mana as romantic, Mana was still his defining emotional and professional bond.

So Haruko’s wish is not merely “I want a boyfriend.” It is closer to:

> **“I want to be chosen first, not as the reliable second person, not as the one who waited, not as the one who supported someone else’s miracle, but as myself.”**

That is deeply consistent with Haruko’s whole character. She spent years being not-Mana, not-Sakura, not-Rei, not-young-enough, not-sparkling-enough. Her romantic wish mirrors her idol wound.

### 4\. Haruko’s love is self-limiting because her kindness resists possession

Haruko’s core virtue is that she does not become bitter. She loves by supporting, feeding, encouraging, remembering, and making space. That is beautiful, but it also makes her bad at claiming things for herself. She can joke “Makino is Haruko’s,” but when the emotions get serious, she tends to retreat into teasing, work, or “manager” language.

So Mana complicates her feelings because Haruko is exactly the kind of person who would hesitate to compete with a dead beloved memory. She would rather be the person who helps Makino keep moving than the person who demands he choose her over the past.

## Final assessment

Haruko’s romantic interest is **stronger than deniable subtext but weaker than explicit canon romance**. Across the transcripts, Makino is her old crush, her emotional witness, her safest adult companion, her date-roleplay partner, her drinking partner, her praise source, her professional anchor, and the person through whom she most often imagines being cared for romantically.

Mana complicates this because Makino is not emotionally “empty space” for Haruko to occupy. He is someone whose life has already been shaped by Mana, and Haruko knows that better than almost anyone. Her feelings therefore carry a quiet pain:

> **Haruko wants to be chosen, but she loves people by not forcing them to choose.**

That is why her romance coding is so poignant. Haruko can joke, flirt, cook, drink, tease, ask for dates, ask for jealousy, and say “Makino is mine” while tipsy — but the deepest wish underneath is much simpler and harder for her to say plainly:

> **She wants the person who has watched her from the beginning to finally see her not only as the reliable Haruko-san, not only as Mana’s surviving friend, not only as Sunny Peace’s older sister, but as the woman he would choose first.**

**Assistant — Turn 36**

This Chisa batch is immediately distinct from both Rei and Haruko. Rei’s cards often ask how discipline becomes warmth. Haruko’s ask how delayed blooming becomes radiance. Chisa’s ask something softer but very central to SUNNY PEACE:

> **How does a girl who sees herself as timid, dependent, and always “trying to catch up” become someone who can support other people’s hearts?**

The emerging thesis is:

> **Shiraishi Chisa is not simply the shy, cute, younger-sister member of SUNNY PEACE. She is an idol whose weakness becomes empathy: because she knows fear, self-doubt, dependence, and the difficulty of continuing, she can stand beside fans and say, “Let’s keep trying together.”**

## 1\. ラヴラビに関われるなんて — Chisa as horror otaku, creative professional, and hidden specialist

This is a very important Chisa file because it foregrounds a side of her that is easy to flatten if one only sees her as “cute younger sister.” Chisa is a serious horror-film enthusiast. Her love for ラヴズ・ラビリンス / ラヴラビ is not shallow fan trivia; she understands directors, motifs, genre atmosphere, psychology, staging, color, creature design, and adaptation fidelity. She talks about the film’s fairy-tale horror balance, its psychological pull, and the importance of reproducing the world faithfully. card\_chs\_001\_st-card-chs-05-ali…

The first major contribution is that Chisa’s “cute timidness” coexists with real specialist enthusiasm. When she gets the ambassador/supervision job for the ラヴラビ haunted-house collaboration, she panics because she admires the director, but once she starts working, she is not passive. She proposes concrete staging ideas: creature placement, jump-scare timing, unsettling color, music, source-faithful design, and an interactive attraction. This shows that Chisa’s fandom can become professional creative input.

The Spanish greeting scene — “ブエナス・タルデス！” — is comic, but meaningful. Chisa over-prepares in an anxious, earnest way. She studies Spanish because the director is Spanish, nearly turns a professional meeting into fan worship, and must be steadied by Makino. Yet the director reads this as 健気 (earnest, devoted, admirably sincere). That is a key Chisa word even when not explicitly thematized: she is clumsy, but her clumsiness often comes from wanting to respect others properly.

The haunted-house inspection also deepens her relationship to fear. Chisa loves horror, but she can still be startled and physically cling to Makino when something jumps out. This is important because it prevents her horror interest from becoming “fearless goth girl” coding. She likes horror not because she is immune to fear, but because she enjoys fear as crafted experience. She can be scared and still appreciate the design.

By the end, the collaboration is successful enough that the director invites her to audition for a film. That is a significant expansion of Chisa’s possible future. Her “weird” interest becomes a professional door.

**Contribution to Chisa:** Very important. This file establishes Chisa as a niche creative specialist: not merely cute, but passionately literate in horror, capable of serious supervision, and able to turn private fandom into idol-adjacent professional identity.

## 2\. 日頃の感謝を歌に乗せて — Chisa’s solo anxiety, fan gratitude, and Saki dependence

This is one of the core Chisa files. It starts from a birthday project where Chisa can choose what she wants to do. Her first instinct is horror — a film appearance, special makeup, short video — but she quickly rejects that because some fans might find it hard to enjoy. That tells us a great deal about her idol philosophy. Chisa has strong personal interests, but when asked what she wants to give fans, she prioritizes their comfort and happiness. card\_chs\_002\_st-card-chs-05-bir…

The key phrase here is 感謝 (gratitude). Chisa wants to repay the fans who supported her, who said they gained courage from watching her work hard. She is not the kind of idol who imagines herself above the audience, bestowing light from a superior position. She sees encouragement as reciprocal: fans encouraged her, so she wants to return that encouragement through song.

Her anxiety appears immediately when the birthday live becomes real. She asks Saki to perform with her as 白石SISTERS because she feels safer beside her older sister. This is the central Chisa wound: she adores Saki, but that adoration easily becomes dependence. She wants to stand with Saki, but part of her also wants Saki to shield her from being alone.

The イマジナリーSISTER sequence is hilarious but psychologically exact. Chisa invents an “imaginary Saki” to coach her through solo practice. Sakura apparently gave her the idea of imitating others so she would not feel alone, but Chisa transforms it into something deeply sister-coded: Saki becomes an internalized voice of correction and support. This is not merely a gag. It shows how much of Chisa’s self-regulation has historically been mediated through Saki. Even when Saki is absent, Chisa creates a Saki inside herself to keep moving.

Saki’s side is equally important. She is watching from nearby, worried but deliberately holding back because she believes Chisa is now a proper idol who can run on her own. The phrase 自慢の妹 (my proud / beloved younger sister, literally “younger sister I can boast of”) matters enormously. Saki does not see Chisa as a burden. She sees her as someone who has grown through SUNNY PEACE and Hoshimi Pro.

The birthday live resolves this beautifully. Chisa performs alone first, carrying her gratitude to fans, then Saki appears as a surprise guest. This is not a regression into dependence. It is a reward after independence. Chisa proves she can stand alone, then sings with Saki not as a child clinging to an older sister, but as 妹として、ライバルとして、一人のアイドルとして — as a younger sister, as a rival, and as an idol in her own right.

**Contribution to Chisa:** Core. This file defines Chisa’s central arc: dependence on Saki becoming internal courage, fan gratitude becoming performance, and “younger sister” becoming compatible with independent idolhood.

## 3\. 慰安旅行 in京都 — Chisa’s fear logic, superstition, and Makino as お守り

This card is lighter than the birthday-live file but very useful for Chisa’s personality. It places her in Kyoto with Saki, Shizuku, Rui, and Suzu, and immediately foregrounds her attraction to パワースポット (spiritual/power spots) and 心霊スポット (haunted/spirit spots). Chisa wants to visit a shrine famous for 丑の刻参り — the old curse ritual involving straw dolls and nails. card\_chs\_003\_st-card-chs-05-bir…

The comedy comes from Chisa being both fascinated by horror and extremely susceptible to ritual anxiety. She explains curses, taboos, shrine etiquette, coins, bells, and divine punishment with increasing intensity. Makino gets nervous, then Chisa herself draws 大凶 (great misfortune) on an omikuji and panics that their 縁 (bonds / relationships) with Hoshimi Pro and Makino might be severed.

That moment matters. What frightens Chisa is not generic supernatural harm. It is separation. She imagines being cut off from 星見プロのみんな and from Makino. So even a comic curse scenario reveals her deepest fear: losing the bonds that make her brave.

The お祓い (ritual purification / exorcism) scene turns Makino into a protective companion. Chisa calms down after they undergo the ritual together, then calls Makino an お守り — a protective charm or amulet — because having him nearby makes her feel protected from scary things. The final line, asking him to keep watching over her and help grant wishes together, is not strongly romantic compared with Haruko’s material, but it is emotionally intimate. Makino functions as safety.

**Contribution to Chisa:** Important texture. It shows that Chisa’s horror love is entangled with real fear, superstition, attachment anxiety, and her need for protective bonds. Horror is fun for her, but only because she has people to return to.

## 4\. 10カワだよ — Chisa through the eyes of SUNNY PEACE

This is a birthday-day ensemble file and very useful for Chisa’s position within SUNNY PEACE. The first section with Rei is especially important because Rei says Chisa’s dance has reached the level where she no longer needs correction in the old sense; differences between Rei and Chisa are now a matter of color or taste. That is a major growth marker. card\_chs\_004\_st-card-chs-05-bir…

Chisa still reflexively says she is “まだまだ” — not there yet, still behind — but Rei frames her as having her own 色や味 (color and flavor). This is exactly the same larger arc from the Saki unit card: Chisa no longer has to become someone else. She must develop her own expression.

Rei’s “original scary story” is adorable because it reverses their usual dynamic. Rei, the serious dancer, tries to give Chisa a horror-themed birthday present. It is not very scary, but Chisa calls it “10カワ” rather than “10怖”: ten-cute instead of ten-scary. That little coinage captures Chisa’s emotional generosity. She evaluates the gift not by technical horror effectiveness, but by the affection and effort behind it.

The Shizuku lunch scene adds more interpersonal warmth. Shizuku and the others prepare a lunch for Chisa; Shizuku awkwardly attempts an あーん (feeding someone by hand) because Nagisa suggested Chisa might like it. Chisa is startled but accepts, then reciprocates. This is not major plot, but it matters because it shows Chisa as receptive to intimacy and capable of gently normalizing other people’s awkward affection.

The live section shows how much the group has internalized Chisa’s growth. Rei watches Chisa perform and remembers how she once lagged behind in running, feared Rei’s strictness, and steadily worked through tours and harsh locations. Rei crying and hugging Chisa after the live is a powerful social confirmation: Chisa’s effort has become visible to the people who once trained beside her.

**Contribution to Chisa:** Important-to-very-important. This file confirms that Chisa’s growth is not only self-perceived; Rei, Shizuku, Mei, and the others now see her as radiant, skilled, lovable, and independent.

## 5\. 物持ちがいいんですね — Chisa as the idol who teaches by having struggled

This is one of the strongest Chisa files conceptually. A fan writes that she started dancing because she admired Chisa and wants Chisa to teach her. Chisa’s first reaction is disbelief: Rei is better at dance, so why Chisa? This is Chisa’s default comparative self-erasure. She cannot easily imagine herself as someone’s “first choice” when a more technically impressive person is nearby. card\_chs\_005\_st-card-chs-05-chi…

The teaching practice with Makino is both comic and revealing. Chisa overcorrects: she gets too close physically, then overcompensates by demonstrating too fast, then panics when asked for improvement points. Her first teaching mode is anxious performance: “I must act like a teacher.” It does not work because she is trying to be an ideal instructor rather than herself.

Makino’s guidance reframes teaching as 寄り添う (to stand beside / accompany / empathize with). He tells her that because she understands the difficulty and importance of 努力 (effort), she can teach in a way that fits the learner’s perspective. This is probably the clearest statement of Chisa’s idol value in the batch. She is not special because she never struggled; she is special because she did.

After the break, Chisa changes her method. Instead of beginning with correction, she asks what the girl is good at and what she likes. She tells her that knowing what she enjoys and can do well matters too, because that makes continuing more fun. This is not merely dance advice. It is Chisa’s life philosophy becoming transmissible: if you only stare at what you lack, you break; if you find what lets you continue, you grow.

The final phrase “一緒に、頑張ろうね” — let’s work hard together — is quintessential Chisa. It is not top-down instruction. It is shared effort. Then the later fan letter confirms that the girl advanced in her competition and learned to enjoy dancing from the heart by not giving up. Chisa realizes she can be 誰かの心の支え — someone’s emotional support.

**Contribution to Chisa:** Core. This file defines Chisa as an empathy-based idol: the girl whose history of struggle lets her support fans who are struggling now.

## 6\. 最高の衣装デザイン — Chisa, Saki, self-erasure, and creative independence

This is probably the other core file in the batch alongside the birthday-live and fan-teaching cards. It begins with Chisa overjoyed to form a unit with Saki, because Saki is her original idol image. Saki’s childhood singing is what made Chisa want to become an idol. That gives their unit deep emotional weight: Chisa is not merely performing with a colleague; she is standing beside the person who first made idolhood seem magical. card\_chs\_006\_st-card-chs-05-chs…

Makino asks Chisa to design the unit costumes. At first, this seems like a confidence-building opportunity, but Chisa’s first designs expose her problem: every design centers Saki. She thinks about Saki’s height, beauty, coolness, favorite flowers, sharpness, and elegance. She wants to make Saki shine. What she does not do is design for herself.

The designer rejecting the drafts as “not Shiraishi Sisters costumes” is narratively perfect. The problem is not lack of skill; it is self-erasure. Chisa thinks support means making Saki shine while she recedes. But Saki is doing the same thing in practice, holding herself back so Chisa can stand out. The sisters are trapped in mutual deference: each loves the other so much that she tries to disappear for the other’s sake.

Makino’s intervention makes the lesson explicit: if neither wants the other to hold back, then Chisa must not hold herself back either. The unit is not Saki plus support-Chisa. It is 二人で一つのユニット — two people forming one unit. That phrase is the file’s heart.

The costume error in the final section is also crucial. Chisa initially wants to accept the wrong design because fixing it would inconvenience others. Makino asks what she wants, and she finally says she does not want to compromise on this outfit. This is an important act of わがまま (self-assertion / selfish request, though here in a healthy sense). For Chisa, asserting her desire is growth.

The final result is a costume that realizes “二人で描いた夢” — the dream the two sisters drew together. After the unit battle, Chisa realizes she may not need to become like Saki. She can become her own kind of idol while still loving and competing with her sister.

**Contribution to Chisa:** Core. This file is the clearest “姉離れ” arc — not rejecting Saki, but separating enough from her to stand beside her as an equal.

## Batch synthesis

Across these six files, Chisa’s character is much richer than a simple “timid younger sister” archetype. Her core domains are becoming clear:

**Chisa as effort-based idol.**
She is not written as naturally overwhelming. Her value comes from trying, failing, trembling, asking for help, and continuing anyway. Because fans see that, they feel encouraged.

**Chisa as empathy transmitter.**
Her idol philosophy is not “look how amazing I am,” but “I know it is hard, so let’s keep going together.” This is why the fan-teaching file matters so much.

**Chisa as Saki’s sister, but not Saki’s shadow.**
Saki is her first idol and emotional anchor. But the best Chisa stories do not sever that bond; they mature it. She becomes 妹として、ライバルとして、一人のアイドルとして — sister, rival, and idol.

**Chisa as niche creative.**
Horror, haunted houses, costume design, sewing/fashion, and performance supervision all show her as a girl with specific creative languages. These interests are not side quirks. They are potential professional identities.

**Chisa as fear-facing rather than fearless.**
She loves horror but still gets scared, loves performance but still panics, wants independence but still reaches for Saki or Makino. Her courage is not absence of fear; it is acting while afraid.

**Chisa as SUNNY PEACE’s “support through vulnerability” member.**
Sakura radiates life, Rei transforms discipline into shared joy, Haruko turns endurance into warmth. Chisa contributes a more fragile but powerful form of encouragement: she makes people feel that their own imperfect effort matters.

## Priority ranking

**Core:**
最高の衣装デザイン; 物持ちがいいんですね; 日頃の感謝を歌に乗せて.

**Very important:**
ラヴラビに関われるなんて.

**Important texture:**
10カワだよ; 慰安旅行 in京都.

None of these are throwaway. Even the lighter Kyoto and 10カワ files add useful dimensions: Chisa’s fear logic, Makino-as-protection, group affection, and how others perceive her growth.

## Updated Chisa thesis

> **Shiraishi Chisa is SUNNY PEACE’s empathy idol: a girl who began by chasing her older sister’s light, trembling before her own inadequacy, and measuring herself against stronger performers, but gradually discovers that her own history of fear and effort is precisely what lets her support others. Her idolhood is built from 寄り添う力 — the power to stand beside someone — rather than overwhelming brilliance. She loves horror because it lets fear become crafted excitement; she loves costumes because they let dreams take visible form; she loves Saki because Saki was her first image of idol radiance; and she loves her fans because their encouragement taught her that struggling honestly can itself become someone else’s courage. Chisa’s growth is not becoming fearless or independent in a hard, solitary sense. It is learning that she can stand alone, then choose to stand with others as an equal.**

**Assistant — Turn 38**

This batch significantly expands Chisa in two directions at once.

First, it strengthens the “Chisa as creator” line: costumes, haunted-house concepts, prank design, styling, sewing, acting, and genre analysis. Second, it shows that her gentleness is not passivity. Chisa is timid, yes, but when she is inside one of her domains — horror, fashion, costumes, performance craft, or helping someone she cares about — she becomes surprisingly assertive, obsessive, brave, and inventive.

The updated batch thesis is:

> **Chisa’s growth is not that she stops being soft. It is that her softness becomes productive. She turns fear into horror literacy, insecurity into empathy, admiration into craft, and dependence into bonds that let her stand more firmly.**

## 1\. 凄い衣装が出来ちゃうかも — Chisa as memory-designer, not just costume-designer

This card builds directly on the earlier Shiraishi Sisters costume card, but the scale is different. In the earlier costume story, Chisa had to learn not to design only around Saki. Here, she has to design for a larger collective: Tsuki no Tempest, SUNNY PEACE, the Hoshimi hometown setting, and the fans who will experience the live. It is a movement from “how do I make my sister shine?” to “how do I make everyone’s shared history visible?” card\_chs\_007\_st-card-chs-05-fes…

At first, Chisa does what she often does: she tries to incorporate everyone’s wishes too literally. Mei wants edible donut elements; Sakura wants the outfit to sparkle like sun and moon; Chisa tries to honor every idea and ends up with a grotesquely overstuffed concept. This is comic, but it reveals her default relational method. Chisa does not want to reject anyone’s feelings. She tries to carry all of them at once, even when that becomes impossible.

Makino’s intervention is important because he redirects her from “collect all opinions” to “define the 想い (omoi, feelings/intention) behind the costume.” Chisa’s breakthrough comes when she revisits 星見市 (Hoshimi City), especially the sea and the running course. She remembers being the one who lagged behind during training, and Saki waiting for her. She remembers childhood, the aquarium, being lost, crying, and Saki making herself ridiculous to make Chisa laugh. The costume idea emerges from memory rather than decoration.

The key line is that she wants fans to “一緒に感じてほしい” — she wants them to feel the group’s experiences together through the costume. This is a major refinement of Chisa’s creative identity. She does not design clothes as mere prettiness. She designs them as emotional vessels.

By the end, the sea motif becomes a way to preserve memory: someday, when fans see the Hoshimi sea, she wants them to remember the live. That line, “思い出してくれるように” — so they will remember — is very Chisa. She is not chasing spectacle for its own sake. She wants performance to become a shared emotional landmark.

**Contribution to Chisa:** Very important. This shows Chisa becoming a serious creative mediator: someone who turns place, memory, group history, and fan experience into visual form.

## 2\. 逆襲のドッキリ企画 — Chisa and Suzu: mutual admiration disguised as failed prank comedy

This file looks like pure comedy, but it is actually very useful for Chisa’s social position outside the Saki axis. The premise is an 逆ドッキリ (gyaku-dokkiri, reverse prank): Chisa and Suzu, usually on the receiving end of surprises, will prank Makino. In practice, the prank attempts collapse repeatedly because Chisa and Suzu are too sincere, too bad at deception, or too tangled in their own affection. card\_chs\_008\_st-card-chs-05-fes…

The first planning scene shows Chisa’s horror-production instincts. Suzu thinks in terms of classic variety-show setups, but Chisa immediately starts warping the premise into horror logic: zombification, spiritual pranks, fear ratings. Her “3怖” and “10怖” scale — roughly “3-scary” and “10-scary” — returns here as a private genre metric. What is merely “a bit scary” to Chisa is traumatizing to Suzu, which creates a fun contrast: Chisa is timid in ordinary social situations, but in horror she becomes the dangerous expert.

The failed prank sequence is also revealing. A fake lottery ticket ends up with Fran. Wasabi sushi gets redirected to kana. Sour wine goes to miho. The girls worry they may be hated by IIIX by the end of the tour. This is comedy, but it shows Chisa’s moral sensitivity: she cannot comfortably detach consequences from people. Even when staging a prank, she worries about collateral emotional damage.

The “fight” prank becomes the emotional core. Chisa is supposed to insult Suzu, but her “insults” become praise: Suzu is unfair because she is cute, clumsy yet reliable, and has a wonderful gap between cuteness and coolness. Suzu fires back that Chisa is unfair because she seems timid but is strong, hardworking, and has encouraged Suzu many times. The prank fails because their underlying affection is too genuine to invert into hostility.

The final live reframes the whole mess as 絆 (kizuna, bond). Chisa says that spending time together, imitating each other, and acting together deepened their connection. This matters because Chisa’s most obvious attachment is Saki, but this file shows her building a peer bond with someone very different from herself. Suzu admires Chisa’s ひたむきな努力 (hitamuki na doryoku, single-minded / earnest effort), while Chisa admires Suzu’s proud forward-facing confidence.

**Contribution to Chisa:** Important. This file expands Chisa’s peer network and shows that her “support through vulnerability” can form reciprocal bonds outside her sister relationship.

## 3\. ここが噂のスタジオですね！ — Chisa’s horror empathy: she tries to make even ghosts smile

This is one of the funniest but also most character-defining Chisa cards. A prank show tries to test whether an idol who professes love of horror will actually be frightened by spiritual phenomena. The production assumes that Chisa’s horror fandom is a pose. It is not. card\_chs\_009\_st-card-chs-05-flo…

The entire dokkiri fails because Chisa experiences horror as wonder, not merely fear. She sees salt, talismans, stains, flickering lights, and “poltergeist” activity, and instead of panicking, she becomes excited: “生ポル” (nama-poru, live poltergeist) is a dream encounter for her. A ghostly phone call makes her worry that someone is sick and needs an ambulance. The “ghost” appears, and Chisa introduces herself as an idol.

That moment is the file’s heart. Chisa says idols make people smile, so she wants to make the ghost smile too. Then she offers a handshake, because fans smile when they shake hands with her. This is absurd, but it is also a perfect expression of Chisa’s idol philosophy. She does not turn off empathy because the other party is frightening. Even a ghost is someone she wants to reach.

The staff expected 撮れ高 (toredaka, usable footage / good TV material) from fear. They instead get footage of Chisa making the ghost actor feel guilty for scaring her. That is exactly what makes Chisa distinct: her kindness disarms the genre.

The final punchline — that what truly scares Chisa is not a ghost but Rei during dance lessons — is also meaningful. It confirms that Chisa’s real fear is not supernatural imagery but disappointing someone she respects, especially someone who has trained and corrected her. Her horror tolerance is high; her interpersonal anxiety remains very real.

**Contribution to Chisa:** Very important. This file defines Chisa’s horror identity: she is not fearless, but she metabolizes fear through curiosity, genre knowledge, and empathy.

## 4\. 宇宙人は本当にいます — Chisa’s openness to wonder, genre imagination, and protective courage

As a crossover-associated card, this should be weighted differently from core main-story material, but it is still revealing. It takes Chisa’s existing taste for horror and strange phenomena and pushes it into science fiction, supernatural ambiguity, and genre belief. She has experienced a “dream” involving the SOS Brigade, Haruhi, Yuki, aliens, and even possibly a beam from her eye. Chisa does not treat it as mere fantasy; she tries to verify it. card\_chs\_010\_st-card-chs-05-hru…

What matters for Chisa is not whether the crossover event is metaphysically “real” in the strictest canon sense. What matters is how she responds. She researches 宇宙人 (uchūjin, aliens), UMA, contact entities, alternate worlds, and the boundary between 夢 (yume, dream) and 現実 (genjitsu, reality). She sews the North High uniform from memory to prove the experience happened. She draws the SOS Brigade logo, badly enough that Makino jokes it looks like “ZOZ団,” and then something impossible apparently happens again.

The most important section is the crisis in the liminal school space. Makino is knocked unconscious, a giant insect threatens them, and Chisa’s response is not to collapse. She decides that even if the beam is uncertain, even if the possibility is tiny, she has to try because Makino needs protection. “{user}さんは、私が守ってみせます” — “I will protect you” — is significant. Chisa’s courage emerges under relational pressure.

The ending is also very Chisa. She appears to have been helped by a “無表情だけど根は優しくて” — expressionless but fundamentally kind — alien-like person, almost certainly referencing Yuki, but she tries to hide it. Then she pivots away from obsessing over the supernatural and says that if they are going to chase a dream, they already have the dream of becoming top idols. This is not a rejection of wonder; it is a re-centering of wonder into idol aspiration.

**Contribution to Chisa:** Important, with crossover caveat. It extends her “fear/wonder” domain from horror to the uncanny and shows that her courage activates when someone she cares about is in danger.

## 5\. オススメの映画 — Chisa’s baseline: horror as courage training, fashion as self-expression, sewing as support

This is a foundational Chisa file and arguably one of the best early statements of who she is. It has three major parts: horror, fashion, and handmade support. card\_chs\_011\_st-card-chs-05-ido…

The horror section is crucial. Chisa admits she loves horror movies across subgenres: occult, psycho-horror, splatter, panic, zombie films. The surprising explanation is that horror makes her feel 前向き (maemuki, positive / forward-looking). Why? Because protagonists face terrifying situations and keep resisting death and despair until the end. This is the key to Chisa’s genre psychology. She likes horror because it dramatizes not giving up.

That connects directly to her idol identity. Chisa herself is an anxious girl who often feels inadequate. Horror gives her an aesthetic language for perseverance: fear is real, but people still keep moving. This makes her horror fandom much more than a quirky contrast gag.

The fashion section gives the second baseline. Chisa loves clothes, styling, fashion magazines, and coordinating outfits, but initially imagines herself as too ちんちくりん (chinchikurin, stubby / short and unimpressive, self-deprecating) to be a model. She prefers to style someone else rather than be seen herself. This repeats the same self-erasure pattern as the Saki costume card: she trusts her taste when applied to others, but not herself as the visible subject.

Her line about becoming able to express her 好き (suki, likes / things she loves) because she met Makino and SUNNY PEACE is a major growth marker. Chisa’s hobbies are not merely private. Idolhood gives her permission to externalize them.

The scrunchie section is beautiful because it turns craft into group belonging. She makes お揃いのシュシュ (osoroi no shushu, matching scrunchies) for SUNNY PEACE, using member colors, because she wants to support everyone in her own way. Then she gives one to Makino too, saying he is also a member of SUNNY PEACE. This is one of the clearest expressions of Chisa’s group concept: SUNNY PEACE is not only five girls onstage; it includes the person who guided and supported them.

**Contribution to Chisa:** Core. This file defines Chisa’s baseline triad: horror as courage, fashion as self-expression, handmade objects as relational support.

## 6\. すみれちゃんが出演した作品 — acting, empathy, and the surprising usefulness of “bad girl” roleplay

This file is very useful because it expands Chisa beyond horror/fashion into acting. It begins with her admiration for Sumire’s film performance: she watches it repeatedly, cries, studies emotional impact, and becomes interested in acting. When an audition arrives for a related special drama, she is frightened but chooses to try because Sumire also faced anxiety and created a performance that reached the heart. card\_chs\_012\_st-card-chs-05-mai…

The role she wins is narratively perfect: a superficially nice but secretly cruel girl who bullies Sumire’s heroine. This is the opposite of Chisa’s ordinary personality. The director says her natural goodness leaks into the performance. That is a wonderful character note. Chisa’s problem as an actor is not lack of sincerity; it is that her sincerity is too kind for malice.

Her first attempt to act cruel makes her cry because she imagines Sumire suffering. That is both funny and diagnostic: Chisa has high empathy, but that empathy initially prevents her from inhabiting the antagonist. Makino’s advice reframes the problem as 役作り (yakuzukuri, role construction): if she can empathize with the victim, perhaps she can learn to understand the role too.

Then Chisa goes all-in. She tries to make the role “降りてくる” — have the role descend into her / take hold of her — but since it does not come naturally, she decides to “go meet it” herself. The result is “bad Chisa”: she insults Makino, orders him around, demands drinks, threatens him with yarn, and becomes so immersed that she has trouble coming back. Makino even notes she is drifting less into pure cruelty and more into ツンデレ (tsundere, prickly but affectionate), which is very funny because Chisa’s “meanness” remains oddly performative and cute.

The performance succeeds because she commits. Afterward, she immediately worries she was too cruel to staff and tries to help clean up, reasserting her real personality. The final joke — that being praised makes her consider becoming a bad girl again — is more than a gag. It shows that roleplay briefly gives Chisa access to assertiveness she normally suppresses. Acting lets her try on selfishness, arrogance, and command without permanently becoming cruel.

**Contribution to Chisa:** Very important. It shows Chisa expanding into acting and reveals that her kindness is both her strength and her limitation; performance gives her a safe way to explore disowned assertiveness.

## Batch synthesis

This batch makes Chisa feel much less like a simple “shy cute girl” and much more like a **genre-brained, craft-oriented, empathy-driven creator**.

Her strongest recurring pattern here is:

> **Chisa begins by thinking her interests are strange or secondary, then discovers they can become professional tools and relational bridges.**

Horror becomes courage training, haunted-house supervision, prank design, and idol outreach to ghosts. Fashion becomes styling, costume design, and memory-preservation. Sewing becomes group support. Acting becomes a way to test parts of herself she normally cannot access. Even supernatural weirdness becomes a way to articulate wonder and protective courage.

Several themes deepen:

**Chisa’s courage is genre-mediated.**
She understands fear through stories. Horror films teach her that frightened people can keep going. Haunted houses become designed emotional experiences. Curses and aliens become frameworks through which she processes uncertainty. She is not irrationally fearless; she is narratively literate in fear.

**Chisa’s creativity is relational.**
She does not create to show off. She creates to support: costumes for Saki, costumes for Hoshimi, scrunchies for SUNNY PEACE, horror attractions for fans, outfits for others, even a ghost handshake because idols make people smile.

**Chisa’s self-confidence remains uneven.**
She can design for others, praise others, help others, and even protect Makino, but she still resists being the model, the center, or the visible subject. This is why her arc remains active: her skill often runs ahead of her self-image.

**Chisa is more assertive inside fiction than in ordinary life.**
When she is acting, designing horror, explaining genre, or protecting someone, she can become surprisingly forceful. Her “bad girl” role exposes an assertive Chisa that ordinary Chisa cannot comfortably own yet.

**Chisa’s bonds keep widening.**
Earlier files centered Saki. This batch gives major weight to SUNNY PEACE as a six-person ecology including Makino, to Suzu as peer partner, to Sumire as acting model, to Rei as terrifying mentor, and to fans/Hoshimi City as memory recipients.

## Priority ranking

**Core:**
オススメの映画. This is foundational for Chisa’s horror philosophy, fashion insecurity, handmade support, and Makino/SUNNY PEACE belonging. card\_chs\_011\_st-card-chs-05-ido…

**Very important:**
凄い衣装が出来ちゃうかも; ここが噂のスタジオですね！; すみれちゃんが出演した作品. These define Chisa as memory-designer, horror-empathy idol, and emerging actress. card\_chs\_007\_st-card-chs-05-fes… card\_chs\_009\_st-card-chs-05-flo… card\_chs\_012\_st-card-chs-05-mai…

**Important:**
逆襲のドッキリ企画; 宇宙人は本当にいます. The former expands her peer bond with Suzu; the latter expands her openness to wonder and protective courage, though with crossover weighting. card\_chs\_008\_st-card-chs-05-fes… card\_chs\_010\_st-card-chs-05-hru…

## Updated Chisa thesis after this batch

> **Shiraishi Chisa is SUNNY PEACE’s empathy-craft idol: a timid but quietly obsessive girl whose “strange” loves — horror films, haunted houses, fashion, sewing, costumes, supernatural stories, and acting — become ways of supporting others. She is not fearless; rather, she understands fear as something people can pass through together. She is not naturally self-assertive; rather, she learns to assert herself through craft, performance, and the desire to protect or encourage someone else. Chisa’s idolhood is built from 寄り添う力 (the power to stand beside someone), but this batch shows that standing beside someone does not mean remaining small. It means turning one’s own fears, tastes, memories, and handmade gifts into forms that help others smile, remember, and keep going.**
